## Researcher Response 44

**Conversion for the full ordinary binary surface $\approx_{A,U,I}$ is decidable on well-typed terms over every finite computad, in every dimension.** The normal form records each occurrence of an operation outside the word signature unless its *generic* type is convertible to one of the defining cases. In particular there is an effective test for whether clause (ii) applies. Even over a globular arity, being a word typed using conversion is insufficient for that test. The result gives a finite family of universal old-boundary problems for each folded surface witness in Q1, without assuming that arbitrary surface witnesses can be rehydrated.

### Normal histories in the free surface algebra

Use the following recursive data for positive-dimensional cells. Store the two lower normal faces and a composable list of events at their top cut, allowing the empty list at a cell. An event is an atom, or an event whiskered at a specified lower cut on a specified side by a stationary normal operand of specified rank. Atoms are ambient variables with their prescribed faces, or individual heads with their arity, normalized generic boundary and complete tuple of normalized arguments. At degree zero keep the ambient object. For each $r$ the normal forms at lower ranks will already be available; normalizing the $r$-arguments of an $r$-head calls the procedure only on proper argument expressions at that rank.

Normalize lists by the directed exchange of Response 42 at **every** cut $j<r-1$. Thus a right event followed by a left event at their first divergence may change places if the two stationary operands are exactly the respective source and target required for that exchange. Change those operands to the opposite ends. Allow the move inside identical exterior whiskers. Comparison uses the already normalized lower cells, and the faces of a whiskered event are given by the lower binary operation. An exterior whisker with a lower-dimensional stationary operand is retained with that dimension. Concatenate and sort for top composition. For a binary operation at a lower cut, execute the left argument's events against the right source and then the right events against the left target; when an operand already has smaller dimension use that stationary operand as it stands. Sort the result. Identities have empty lists.

Sorting terminates since a directed exchange removes an inversion of binary addresses. It is confluent on lists, without any assumption that lower composition has unique factors. Disjoint temporal redexes commute. At an overlapping pair the three addresses decrease lexicographically, with adjacent addresses prefix-incomparable. At their first split one side has two events and the other one. Above it the stationary labels and tags on the three events agree by the overlap tests. The two events on the same side can be exchanged there and the third can pass each, since their internal exchange preserves the outside ends. This supplies the joining hexagon including the corrected stationary labels. Induction on the inversion number proves uniqueness. It also shows that each exchange class is effectively **finite**: from the normal list follow directed moves backwards. The addresses are permuted, their number is fixed, and each reverse move strictly increases inversion number. At a chosen adjacent pair the divergence is prescribed, the two inner events are retained and the earlier stationary operands, if legal, are determined by their faces. There are only finitely many such reverse paths. [Labelled exchange and its overlapping cases](#researcher-response-5) · [More than one lower cut](#researcher-response-42)

For a head of rank $r$ normalize its generic faces in its own globular arity $P$. Apply these three rules in order.

1. If its arity and normalized generic boundary are those of a designated binary operation of that rank, apply the binary prescription above. The two maximal disc positions, their dimensions and their divergence determine the operation. The chosen boundary is compared *after* normalization.
2. If its generic faces agree as a normal cell $z$ and $z$ has a representative which is a **raw word on $P$**, return the empty history on the evaluated face. This includes the designated identity. The raw-word test is given below.
3. Otherwise use one head atom with the displayed generic data and argument tuple. Its actual faces are obtained by evaluating the two generic face expressions on that tuple.

These rules give exactly the full binary quotient. For soundness, substitution expands each input event in its stated exterior context, substituting into stationary labels and opaque head arguments. An exchange expands to the rectangle of exchanges of the two blocks, including an empty rectangle for an identity. The decision about a bound head is made before labelling. Thus performing the evaluations before or after substitution gives the same classes. In the simultaneous induction on formation and conversion this also shows that a congruence in a head type preserves its rule and result.

For (i) at rank $r$, work first on the generic raw-parallel words. At each active $(r-1)$-variable leaf of the common literal source there is a path of generating $r$-steps. It is determined by the common strict value: distinct such leaves are distinct transport slots and the top chain specifies the steps on each directed string, including the choice between parallel alternatives. The binary boundary formulas retain the surrounding cut tree at every active leaf. A top composition joins corresponding lists through the *literal* common intermediate tree. An identity contributes no step. Thus the lists at each leaf and the stationary subtrees are the same for both words. In either execution the opposite stationary label at each event is the value of the opposite subtree after its already executed prefixes. Sorting at successive binary nodes therefore identifies both histories with the same reading. For $r=1$ this is ordinary concatenation. The argument for slot uniqueness is given in [Response 33](#researcher-response-33). For (ii) the words have the same normal value by (i), and either one supplies the required raw word for rule 2. An ordinary total involving an $r$-variable cannot have equal generic strict faces. Thus rule 2 and binary execution agree wherever an identity is required.

For completeness, each computation has an expression in the surface equal to its input. At rule 1 use congruence of the bound type. At rule 2 take a raw word $w$ representing the common generic face. The inductive completeness at rank $r-1$ gives equality of each generic face to $w$. Its strict value has the required full support, since it is the same strict value as the admissible end face on $P$. The head on $P$ at $w\to w$ exists; congruence followed by (ii) for the reflexive equation $w=w$ gives the designated identity. Rule 3 just records the original head, with congruent bound data and arguments. Expand each binary composite of two rank-$r$ operands at a lower cut into its serial whiskered expression. Distribute an exterior whisker over top composition and over an empty history. These are (i) instances: in the two-disc, whiskered-string and whiskered-identity arities respectively the displayed words have literally the same two faces and the same ordinary strict value. Use the top associativity and top unit instances to read any list with a fixed bracketing. Each directed exchange is (i) at its two-disc arity, also inside an exterior context by that distributivity. A one-event history can be read as the event itself by the top unit laws. All these equalities specialize to tuples typed with conversion. Induction on rank and on the nested atoms and arguments now identifies any two readouts of the same normal data. This proves both directions of the comparison, including for arbitrary attaching terms in the ambient computad. It also explains why a nonbinary head can depend on an argument's *surface class* even when no raw identity-class comparison is available there, as in Response 43.

### Deciding whether a generic face has a raw word representative

This test takes place on a **globular** $P$, so a variable's positive faces are literal variables. We describe it with simultaneous literal seam requirements since matching normalized faces is insufficient. Word syntax here has just variable, identity and indexed binary constructors. On well-typed words this tree specifies the original raw term uniquely and can be recovered from it: a binary head has its specified two maximal arguments, and all its other labels are their iterated faces.

For an unknown raw word of degree $l$ with prescribed normal value $H$, construct a finite expression template with placeholders of *strictly smaller* rank, each with its prescribed normal value. This construction can preserve both literal faces of any solution.

* At rank zero select the variable. For an empty history use the identity on an unknown for its lower value. Any raw word giving the empty history has no active $l$-variable, since each such occurrence contributes an event; its two literal faces are the same lower word. Choosing that word for the unknown preserves both.
* In a nonempty history every atom must be an $l$-variable. Form the binary tree of the addresses of these atoms. An active leaf is a variable position in the lower literal tree; an address cannot both stop and continue. The cut tag at each internal node must be consistent. At each wholly stationary child introduce one lower word unknown, with the rank and value in the opposite events' stationary data. At an active leaf start with the literal source variable of its first event.
* Follow the normal list. Each step must start at the current literal variable of its leaf; put its $l$-variable there inside the tree to obtain the step expression, then update the leaf to the target variable. Top-compose these expressions in their given order. Reject incompatible addresses, illegal cuts or mismatched normalized evaluations, including any inconsistent stationary data. Impose the raw incidence equations for each binary node. A checked assignment of the lower unknowns gives an actual raw word with value $H$.

For necessity start with any raw word giving $H$. Expand it to serial steps on the literal tree of its lower source as in the proof of (i). Sorting these steps is possible on that same literal tree: swapping two independent leaves updates exactly their own variables, with the other subtrees read in their current states. A wholly stationary child never changes its literal writing; a word with no active variables has identical literal faces by the binary boundary formulas. The final tree is precisely the original target. Thus the sorted template admits the original stationary writings, reproduces the normal value, and has the original two literal faces. This statement concerns a word on the generic globular set. An arbitrary declared $l$-cell in a computad can have composite faces.

To solve the resulting finite constraints, work down in rank. Unknowns have prescribed normal values throughout. Keep any needed immediate faces of an unknown as new lower unknowns with the corresponding prescribed values. On constructor trees use the raw boundary formulas. For example a top composite selects the appropriate outside face; a lower cut retains its node and takes the faces of the top-dimensional operands. Raw composability at a cut imposes equality between the appropriate iterated faces. At the current largest rank unify the required literal term equalities by first-order constructor matching and variable binding, rejecting an occurrence of a variable properly inside its own binding. Check the prescribed normal values at each binding, and retain equalities of smaller rank for the next stages. Constructor matching and the occurs check here are on finite *raw* trees, even if both sides have identical normal values. Choose the template above for each still free unknown of this rank. Substitute these choices through all bindings, impose their literal incidence equations and identify their computed faces with any previously requested face unknowns. Only smaller-rank problems remain. In rank zero one compares the actual object variables.

This terminates by rank after finite syntactic unification at each stage. A solution of the constraints gives the requested representative by substitution. Conversely, syntactic unification retains any given solution; at a free unknown replace its word by the template using its stationary subwords. This preserves its normal value and both its literal faces. In particular it preserves all boundary constraints on a forced constructor tree in which it occurs. Literal equalities of the current rank already share their bindings, so the change is made identically on both sides. Induction down the ranks proves that a witness is found whenever one exists. The recursion with the main decoder is well founded: an $r$-head tests raw-word existence only at rank $r-1$, while testing a rank-$l$ template uses only word operations on already prescribed cells and normalizations in lower ranks.

For an example of the distinction, take a vertical pair of globular 2-variables $a:f\to g$, $b:g\to h$. Whisker $a$ on its left by the unit at the source object, then top-compose with $b$ using the unit conversion on $g$. The first event is at a right-child address and the second at the root. No literal source word can have both an active root leaf and an active child leaf. This surface word therefore has no raw **word** representative. The generic reflexive head on its class is an opaque atom under rule 3. If instead the entire pair is left-whiskered, the two events lie at the same right-child leaf and the literal representative exists.

### Finite tests over a folded witness

Fix an output degree $d$. Fold the degrees through $d$ into the terminal raw computad $T_d$ of Response 34. Fix there an old raw type and a full-binary surface class at that type. There is a computable **finite family** of finite presentations with such a pair, mapping to this terminal pair, through which every generator-preserving realization factors. Here we only require existence of a factorization; no choice of a unique presentation up to isomorphism is needed.

To see finiteness, recover the possible shapes of a normal history prior to folding. Relabelling preserves the rank, the decisions on each bound arity, all atom constructors, and the address of every event. It may require sorting after the labels have been relabelled. From the specified normal list enumerate the finite reverse-sort possibilities above. Each such list specifies the folded normal values of all the stationary labels and the names or bound operations and labelled tuples of its atoms. Recover possibilities for these in turn. At the same rank recurse only into arguments of a head atom: their maximum nesting depth of rank-$d$ head atoms is strictly smaller than that of the enclosing atom (use the current rank in place of $d$ at each recursive stage). Sorting never moves an event into or out of an atom. Endpoints and stationary operands have smaller rank. Thus there are finitely many finite syntax templates. They can be spelled by a top-bracketed list of whiskered atoms, placing a word representative nowhere except where the decoder actually calls for one. A bound head can use any surface spelling of its generic data that formed that atom; its arity and its bound operations are independent of the ambient labels. An effective choice is to keep the spelling at its first occurrence during normalization. Alternatively enumerate well-typed generic heads until the indicated data are found, which terminates for an atom arising here.

In these templates and the old type give ambient variable occurrences placeholders. Expand the terminal raw declarations of the placeholders down in rank, as in Response 34. Enumerate the finitely many partitions of these positions respecting their terminal names. For each partition form the candidate computad, retaining it only if declarations agree literally for identified generators, raw typing succeeds, the spelled surface expression typechecks at the old boundary and folding has the specified normal value. Surface typing at this step uses the original decidable arity condition and the conversion test just proved. Candidate histories can also be checked by direct evaluation. Every retained candidate is a realization. Conversely, starting from an actual realization, fold its normal history before its last sort. This gives one of the enumerated reverse-sort lists and recursively one of the enumerated templates. Using the actual names and their raw declarations gives a retained partition and a generator-preserving map back that realizes the original pair. For a head whose surface spelling differs, equality of the generic data and argument tuple gives the same cell by completeness. Finiteness does not assume that sorting decisions reflect along identifications of generator names.

It follows that universal degree-$d$ conservativity for the maximal ordinary binary surface is equivalent to raw inhabitation of the distinguished old type in every member of these families. A raw filler there specializes to a raw filler at the original literal boundary. A failing family member is itself a finite counterexample. The same argument with prescribed normal value gives the test for exact rehydration, which already fails by Response 43. The reduction retains arbitrary dimensions and weak attaching terms; deciding conversion and reducing each fixed witness to finite templates still leave the existence of raw inhabitants for *all* those templates open. [Terminal computads and the published normal-form test](#researcher-response-34) ∎

**Validation.** The implementation reconstructed 2,077 typed raw words, comparing 1,389 additional readings at equal strict top chains and literal boundaries. It tested all 18 orders in three mixed binary pastes, through rank four, and checked each reconstructed word by raw inference and renormalization. It distinguished eligible and ineligible endo heads, respected conversion in the type of a binary head, typechecked the conversion-only example above while rejecting a different generating seam, solved the four left/right seam constraints, rejected a cyclic literal binding and followed a generator at composite attaching faces through a fold. The safe ordinary Agda kernel checked the representative shuffle words and the alternative binary heads at their specified raw types. The general decision and finite-family theorems are paper proofs. ## Researcher Response 45

**A specified surface cell can be lifted up to an invertible cell at the original raw boundary in ordinary CaTT through dimension three, in every finite context.** More generally, let $L$ be any of the eight ordinary binary theories or either published theory. Write $H_X$ for the free raw algebra, $Q_X$ for the raw reduct of $LX$, and $p_X:H_X\to Q_X$ for the canonical map. Let $B=X_{\le r}$, $r\ge1$. If $p_B$ is a raw weak equivalence, the lifting assertion holds through dimension $r+2$. It includes noninvertible surface cells and arbitrary higher declarations. There is a relative version with **no hypothesis on $B$**. Exact representation has a different answer. For each binary theory, in either kernel, some surface cells have no raw representative at *any* boundary. The least possible degree of this phenomenon is two when $A$ or $U$ is present, and three otherwise.

### Lifting the specified cell in a relative extension

Put $n=r+1$ and $Y=Q_B\amalg_{H_B}H_X$. The raw map $q:Y\to Q_X$ is the identity on $Q_B$ through degree $r$, using the canonical copies on both sides. Indeed no head of degree at most $r$ uses a slot of larger dimension, the equations at these degrees use only these data, and higher declarations do not change lower equality. The claim is that for $k\le n+1$, parallel $u,v\in Y_{k-1}$ and **any** $c:qu\to qv$ in $Q_X$, there is $b:u\to v$ in $Y$ and a coinductively invertible cell $qb\to c$ with those fixed faces. In degree zero take the vertex itself.

We give the arity calculation used below. For fixed parallel $(n-1)$-faces of a raw algebra $C$, form the category with the $n$-cells at these faces as objects. Its arrows are $(n+1)$-cells modulo an invertible $(n+2)$-cell at fixed ends. Denote it by $D(C)$, with the faces understood. Top composition and identity descend: paste comparisons in the varying slots and use the structural associator and unit comparisons at the top-composition arities. Those structural comparisons are invertible by lifting over the identity strict cell on the arity, recursively lifting the inverse and its cancellation cells. This also makes the relation transitive. An isomorphism is represented by an invertible cell: choose an arrow representing its inverse; the two equalities of arrow classes supply invertible cancellation cells. Raw maps induce functors. [Closure of weak invertibility under globular pasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2303.14907)

For a globular tree $P$ write
$$
  W(P)=Q_{P_{\le r}}\amalg_{H_{P_{\le r}}}H_P,qquad V(P)=Q_P .
$$
Both project by globular trivial fibrations to the ordinary strict $FP$. For $V$ this is Response 32. For $W$, push out the weak equivalence $p_{P_{\le r}}$ along the cellular inclusion. Then $W(P)\to FP$ is a weak equivalence by two-out-of-three, is onto vertices and has gaunt target, giving the stated lifting by the arity criterion of Response 31. There is a raw comparison $w:W(P)\to V(P)$ over $FP$. A globular labelling of $P$ in $Y$ specializes $W(P)\to Y$: on the low slots extend by surface freeness into $Q_B$, then use the labels of the remaining generators. The corresponding $V(P)\to Q_X$ at the projected tuple commutes with $w$. In either generic algebra any two parallel cells over the same strict value have an invertible comparison over the identity of that value, by the recursive lifting just described. In particular a specified strict arrow with specified lifts of its ends has a **unique arrow class** in $D$ above it. This uniqueness is used on generic trees before specializing. [Generic base change and the gaunt-target criterion](#researcher-response-31) · [Globular comparisons for all ten surfaces](#researcher-response-32)

Here are two consequences of this calculation that keep track of the comparison after a change of labels.

1. On a tree $T$ of height at most $n$, hold all labels below $n$ fixed. A generic $n$-cell in $W(T)$ or $V(T)$ acts functorially on the $n$-labels in the categories $D$. To act on arrows, replace each varying $n$-disc by two parallel copies joined by an $(n+1)$-disc. Its two end inclusions agree on the lower tree. The strict value of the generic $n$-operation is a strict paste of its slots, possibly on a subarity. Apply that same strict paste to the slot arrows, using the top composition of independent moves or, equivalently, strict interchange. Lift this strict arrow on the raised tree between the two included operations. Its class is unique there. Specialize to the actual arrows. First make this construction in $W$. Changing one arrow by an invertible higher cell gives the same class: replace that raised slot by a globe on the two parallel arrows, leaving the other arrows stationary. Lift the pasted strict $(n+2)$-cell between the two chosen arrows on this tree. Its specialization is invertible. Indeed a relative term there uses raw operations, variables and constants of the low surface base. Every base cell in degree $n+2$ is invertible, since its projection to the free strict category on the low skeleton is an identity and the projection lifts globes. The only variable of degree $n+2$ has the given invertible label. Induction on the relative term now uses closure of invertibility under raw pasting; lower-dimensional arguments can be arbitrary. For a $V$-operation choose a $W$-operation at the same lower faces and strict value. Its image is related to the given operation by the generic invertible comparison over the strict identity. On the raised tree, the chosen arrow for the $V$-operation agrees in $D$ with the arrow for the $W$-operation conjugated by the two end comparisons, by the same strict value and ends. This gives independence of arrow representatives also in $V$. To compare substitution by identities, fold the raised slots to identities on $T$; for two consecutive moves use the tree with a two-step string at each moving slot. There the single lift and the composite lift are parallel over the same strict value. Their classes agree. This proves the identity and composition laws, also when some inputs are stationary. For successive operations the same computation on the raised input tree compares the single lift for the substituted operation to the paste of the individual lifts. The pastes have the same strict value by strict substitution.
2. If $s,t$ are generic $n$-operations on $T$ at the same lower faces and over the same strict value, the generic comparison over the identity gives a natural isomorphism between their actions. Its naturality square consists of two arrows on the raised tree with equal strict value and equal ends. The preceding uniqueness proves commutativity. The same argument compares $w$ of the action of a $W$-operation with the action of its image in $V$. Now take an $(n+1)$-head on $P$. The $n$-slots there occur in directed strings of parallel slots joined by $(n+1)$-generators, allowing a single stationary slot. Collapsing each string to one slot gives its truncated tree $T$; take its first and last sections. The source and target $n$-operations are supported on these sections, since their strict values are the signed boundaries, or the same supported value for an identity head. They restrict to operations $s,t$ on $T$ with the same strict value and lower faces. In $D(V(P))$ the arrow of the head equals the action of $s$ on the strings of input arrows, followed by the structural isomorphism from $s$ to $t$ on the final tuple. Both arrows on $P$ have the given ends and the designated strict value. With no moving slots this just says the head is the structural comparison. Consequently, replacing the objects at the $n$-slots by isomorphic objects and conjugating each input arrow by those isomorphisms conjugates the output arrow by the actions at the two ends. This follows by functoriality along each string and naturality of the final comparison. For expressions using a proper support apply the construction on that support and include it into $P$; the restrictions of the expressions exist by the word retraction of Response 27. [Positional support](#researcher-response-27) · [Transport strings in an ordinary globular tree](#researcher-response-33)

Construct a surface algebra over $Q_X$ as follows. Through degree $r$ take the common lower cells. In degree $n$, over $t$ take pairs $(y,j)$ where $y$ is a cell of $Y$ at the given faces and $j:qy\cong t$ is an isomorphism in $D(Q_X)$. Identify $(y,j)$ with $(y',j')$ if there is an isomorphism $i:y\cong y'$ in $D(Y)$ with $j'\,q(i)=j$. Composition and inversion show that this is an equivalence relation. At two such classes over $t,t'$ admit the actual $(n+1)$-cell $c:t\to t'$ precisely when, for representatives of the classes, there is an arrow $a:y\to y'$ in $D(Y)$ satisfying
$$
                             [c]j=j' q(a).                         \tag{9}
$$
Conjugating $a$ by the witnessing isomorphisms makes this independent of the representatives. Above that degree admit one lift of each cell of $Q_X$ at each compatible boundary.

For a degree-$n$ head on $T$, choose $b$ in $W(T)$ with its strict value at its **exact** generic lower faces. These faces already belong to $Q_{T_{\le r}}$. In $V(T)$ let $e:wb\cong h$ be the structural isomorphism to the given head $h$. On an input tuple with representatives $(y_i,j_i)$, the output over $h[t_i]$ is represented by
$$
                 \bigl(b[y_i],\ h[(j_i)]\, e[(qy_i)]\bigr).
$$
The bracket on the tuple of arrows denotes the functor in step 1. A change of $b$ gives an isomorphism on the $W$-arity over the strict identity, whose image respects the two $e$'s by uniqueness in $D(V(T))$. A change of input representatives gives the required isomorphism by applying the action of $b$ to their witnessing isomorphisms and using step 2. Thus the class is well defined.

More generally the displayed formula holds for an entire degree-$n$ expression on a tree, using any lift of its strict value at its lower faces in the corresponding $W$. For a variable take the variable and identity comparison. At a nested application, the generic lifts of the argument expressions define a substitution into $W$; on the low base it is the free surface substitution. The substituted operation is a lift for the full expression. The composite of its structural comparisons agrees with the single structural comparison in $D(V)$ because both have the same generic ends and identity strict value. Step 1 for successive operations and step 2 for the argument isomorphisms give the formula after specialization. This proves the assertion by induction, including the formula for the two boundary expressions used in a newly formed higher head.

In particular two equal generic surface expressions on a tree give the same operation on these classes. Choose their lifts at the common lower faces and compare them by the strict-identity isomorphism in $W$. Its image respects the two structural comparisons in $V$, and the actions of the equal surface expressions on the $j_i$ coincide by the raised-tree calculation. This checks the degree-$n$ equations on the common arity of each defining rule, including clause (ii), insertion on its joined tree, and pruning on its pruned tree. For disc and endo-coherence removal use the disc and the given arity respectively. Specialization of this equality to any tuple gives the required instance. The same check makes the operation independent of conversion in a bound head type.

For an $(n+1)$-head choose generic $W$-lifts for its two boundary expressions on their sections. Lift its designated strict $(n+1)$-value in $W(P)$ at those faces. On the generic tuple the arrow so obtained satisfies (9) with the structural isomorphisms at the two ends: this is equality over a single strict arrow in $D(V(P))$. For an actual tuple choose representatives at every $n$-slot, and actual $Y$-representatives of the witnessing arrows at its $(n+1)$-slots. They label $P$ in $Y$, so specialize the chosen lift along $W(P)\to Y$. At the projected tuple the generic equation still holds. To obtain the required equation for the **actual** surface input, apply step 2 along the strings. Each projected input arrow conjugates to its actual arrow by (9), so the two extra outside isomorphisms are exactly the actions on the $j_i$ in the displayed formula. This proves closure at the required cell $c$. At degree $n+1$ and above, cells are determined by their projected cell and chosen boundary, so the equations follow from those in $Q_X$ and the boundary computations. This gives the surface algebra by simultaneous interpretation of formation and conversion.

Its restriction on the base has a tautological assignment: a base $n$-cell $y$ takes the class $(y,1)$ and a base $(n+1)$-cell witnesses (9) by itself. To check the $n$-operation for such a tuple, the specialization $W(T)\to Y$ factors through $V(T)\to Q_B\to Y$. Thus the structural comparison specializes **in $Y$** to an isomorphism respecting the two representatives. At degree $n+1$ use the base cell at these tautological representatives. More generally every raw head on a tuple of tautological cells has its tautological value: in degree $n$ its own raw head provides the generic lift, and in degree $n+1$ its own value witnesses (9). Assign every higher generator of $X$ its tautological lift, using uniqueness over its boundary above $n+1$. Freeness now gives a section over $LX$. By the base and raw-head computations the section takes every $qy$ for a relative raw $n$-term $y$ to its tautological class. For a specified degree-$n$ cell read its pair in the section. For a degree-$(n+1)$ cell at relative raw faces use (9) at their tautological representatives. Equality of the two arrow classes there is exactly an invertible $(n+2)$-cell between the projected witness and the specified cell. This proves the relative claim.

If $p_B$ is a weak equivalence, left properness makes $H_X\to Y$ a weak equivalence. Local essential surjectivity at the fixed original raw faces gives a raw representative of the chosen relative witness up to an invertible cell at those faces. Its image under $q$ followed by the relative comparison proves the assertion about $p_X$. For $r=1$ the base is a graph and its comparison is a weak equivalence by Response 32. The same reasoning applies whenever the generating $r$-skeleton is globular. In categorical terms, at fixed original $(n-1)$-faces the induced functor on $D$ is full and essentially surjective. Thus a whole graph of specified surface arrows can be lifted together up to isomorphism: choose a representative and isomorphism once at each vertex, conjugate each edge by those choices, and use fullness at its chosen ends. [Fixed-hom lifting and base change](#researcher-response-31) · [Weak equivalences and local essential surjectivity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.13240)

### Reducing a first failure to the top homotopy groups of a skeleton

Write $E_d(f)$ for lifting **specified** cells up to an invertible cell at every original boundary through degree $d$. Write $C_d(f)$ for reflection of the existence of a degree-$d$ cell at each original boundary. For a raw map between fibrant models, $E_d$ is the derived globe lifting condition through $d$ in Response 33. Indeed the framed mapping spaces out of a globe restrict by fibrations to the mapping spaces out of its boundary. The space of lifts and the space of squares both fibre over the source model's space of boundaries. On each such fibre, surjectivity on components is exactly lifting a specified cell up to a path at the fixed boundary. A path there is equivalence in the fixed hom. Surjectivity on total components is equivalent to fibrewise surjectivity here: transport a preimage along the projected path in the base and use path lifting. In particular the assertion and its version asking only for nonempty fibres transfer across weak equivalences of source models.

For $d\ge4$ put $B=X_{\le d-2}$. Then
$$
\begin{aligned}
 E_d(p_B)&\ \Longrightarrow\ E_d(p_X),\\
 E_{d-1}(p_B)\ \text{and}\ C_d(p_B)&\ \Longrightarrow\ C_d(p_X).
\end{aligned}                                                       \tag{11}
$$
For the first implication use the factorization of Response 33 with $Q=LB$. In its notation $H_X\to W$ and $W'\to Y$ are weak equivalences and $W\to W'$ is bijective through $d$. Thus they lift a **specified** $Y$-cell up to equivalence at the original boundary: first lift along $W'\to Y$ up to equivalence, read the resulting cell in $W$ by the bijection, and lift along $H_X\to W$ up to equivalence. Compose its two comparisons in $Y$. Apply the relative theorem for $q$ with $r=d-2$.

For the second implication factor $p_B$ into a trivial cofibration $H_B\to E$ and a fibration $E\to Q_B$. This fibration lifts globes literally through $d-1$ by $E_{d-1}$, and it reflects existence at its degree-$d$ boundaries by $C_d$ and the mapping-space argument above. For every degree-$d$ lifting square adjoin to $E$ a generator mapping to the specified bottom cell. Each new generator has an **existing** filler in $E$ at its attaching boundary, so this extension $E\to E^+$ admits a raw retraction over $E$ (the retraction need not respect the map to $Q_B$). One stage solves all such squares because it adds no $(d-1)$-cells. Next attach only generators of degrees greater than $d$ to reach a trivial fibration $E'\to Q_B$. Push out these extensions along $E\to W=E\amalg_{H_B}H_X$. In the resulting sequence $W\to W^+\to W'$ the first map has a raw retraction fixing the images of the original declarations and the second is bijective through $d$. As before $W'\to Y$ is a weak equivalence by left properness. Given a surface inhabitant at a degree-$d$ old boundary, lift it up to equivalence to $Y$ by the relative theorem. Reflect existence into $W'$, take its literal representative in $W^+$, retract to $W$, and reflect into $H_X$. This proves the second implication without requiring the retraction to preserve the specified output class. [Raw factorizations and their attachment maps](#researcher-response-33)

Both hypotheses in (11) have a low homotopy-group test. For an $m$-generated computad $B$ (generators of degree at most $m$), $m\ge2$, fix parallel old $(m-1)$-faces. Let $K$ and $K'$ be the spaces of $m$-cells at these faces in $H_B$ and $Q_B$, respectively, taking the invertible higher cells in that hom. Every cell of degree greater than $m$ in these models is invertible. In the raw algebra this follows by induction since there are no generators in those degrees. For a surface expression at such a degree apply step 2 with its top input arrows already invertible by induction: a functor sends their composites to isomorphisms and the final structural comparison is invertible. That generic calculation specializes into any surface algebra. Thus all the high cells relevant to lifting can be read in these spaces. For every raw $m$-cell $x$ at the poles, use $x,p_Bx$ as basepoints. Then

* $C_{m+2}(p_B)$ holds exactly when $\pi_1(K,x)\to\pi_1(K',p_Bx)$ is injective at all these choices.
* Specified lifting in degree $m+2$ holds exactly when this injectivity holds and $\pi_2(K,x)\to\pi_2(K',p_Bx)$ is surjective at all these choices.

Indeed a boundary in degree $m+2$ is two paths with the same endpoints. A homotopy between their images says their difference loop is trivial there. This gives the first equivalence, including necessity by taking the other path constant. At a fillable boundary the homotopy classes of fillings rel boundary form a torsor for $\pi_2(K,x)$: glue a filling to the reverse of a fixed one, or insert a based sphere into that filling. This action is natural for the map of fixed homs. It proves sufficiency for specified lifting and necessity by taking both paths constant at $x$. Here paths and homotopies at old faces can be represented by actual cells by the fixed-hom and globe-fibre comparisons, which apply to the raw reduct on either side. [Globular fibres and homotopy groups](#researcher-response-33)

Consequently universal specified lifting through $d$ for a given ordinary surface is **equivalent** to these $\pi_1$ and $\pi_2$ tests on all finite $m$-generated computads for $2\le m\le d-2$. Necessity uses the computads themselves. For sufficiency start with degree three above, induct on output degree, and use the two group tests for the next degree on its $(d-2)$-skeleton, followed by the first implication of (11). For Q1 itself, assuming universal specified lifting through $d-1$, universal conservativity in degree $d$ is equivalent to just the injectivity test on the $(d-2)$-skeleta, by the second implication. In particular universal specified lifting through four is equivalent to vanishing of $\pi_2(K')$ at the raw components on all finite ordinary 2-computads: $K$ is aspherical there by the planar calculation, and injectivity already follows from degree-four conservativity. If that vanishing holds for a surface, a degree-five failure of its Q1 is already witnessed on a 3-computad. This last conclusion is conditional; the planar calculation for the raw model does not compute the second homotopy group of its free surface reduct. [Planar fixed homs](#researcher-response-10) · [Degree-four conservativity](#researcher-response-6)

There is also a degree-five test that avoids computing the free surface on a 2-computad. For the chosen surface let $R$ be a planar surface model of the 2-skeleton $B_2$ equipped with a raw weak equivalence $H_{B_2}\to UR$, as in Response 26. For $B_3=X_{\le3}$ freely adjoin its 3-generators **in surface algebras** to $R$ at their given images, obtaining $R_3$. Write $f_3:H_{B_3}\to UR_3$. Then $E_4(f_3)$ holds. Indeed the relative construction works with $R$ in place of $LB$ and its free surface extension in place of $LX$: no higher generator enters a term, type or equality through rank $r$, so the common low data are those of $R$. The generic algebras $W(P),V(P)$ and their strict projections are unchanged. On a tuple of base labels both specialization maps now factor through the given action on $R$ as in the tautological-class check. This supplies a surface map from $R$ into the algebra of classes over the extension; its composite with projection is the base inclusion. The free surface extension property gives the section after choosing the tautological lifts of the fresh generators. On the raw side left properness still gives a weak equivalence out of the original free raw algebra. Apply this argument with $r=2$.

Consequently if a degree-five old type of $X$ witnesses a failure of the chosen surface, **some old 5-type of $B_3$ has an inhabitant in $R_3$ and none in $H_{B_3}$**. To prove the contrapositive, apply the second factorization in (11) to $f_3$ with $d=5$. The assumed reflection on $B_3$ gives the retraction at rank five. Adjoin the remaining generators on the raw side and use the relative theorem on the surface side starting with $R_3$ at $r=3$. Every original surface witness maps to that surface extension by freeness. This proves reflection in $X$. The lower-skeleton witness asserted here is in the indicated free extension of the planar model; it need not have a derivation in $LB_3$. [Construction of the planar models](#researcher-response-26)

### A surface cell outside the entire raw image

For a binary theory use the literal multisets $M$ of Response 43 at rank $k$. Allow an arbitrary finite computad. In the counting model there give all $(k+1)$-generators grade **zero**, and independently count each such generator in its own coordinate by the sum over top slots. These counts define a surface model by the same strict top-multiplicity check. Write $N_g(e)$ for the count of generator $g$. For any raw $(k+1)$-cell $e:s\to t$ of grade zero there is an equality in the free abelian group on literal marked terms:
$$
 M(t)-M(s)=\sum_g N_g(e)\bigl(M(tg)-M(sg)\bigr).                 \tag{10}
$$
For a variable this is its own summand. At a head all top labels have grade zero and both generic faces are mark-free. Hence their marked multisets after labelling are the sums of the label multisets against the two strict $k$-chains, as in (8). The difference of the chains is the boundary of the strict $(k+1)$-chain. For an ordinary head this top chain is the sum over all its top slots, or zero for an identity. For a Gray head use its ordinary strict projection, with the same slot counts. Pairing with the label multisets and applying the induction hypothesis at each top slot proves (10). A mark-free surface boundary has only mark-free raw representatives: degree-$k$ evaluation in the counting model with zero on generators counts precisely the cardinality of $M$.

On the point take distinct parallel surface-equal raw $k$-terms $a,b$ from Response 43, with a common endomorphism type. Let $z$ be the identity on their lower source. It has this type and $M(z)=0$. With the unary operation $d$ of that response, add just two declarations
$$
                        g:z\to d(a),\qquad h:d(b)\to z .
$$
The surface loop $c=g*_{k}h$ is typed by conversion. Every positive top power of $c$, with any fixed bracketing, has grade zero, mark-free outside faces and equal positive counts $m$ of $g,h$. Were it represented by a raw cell at **any** faces, the left side of (10) would vanish while the right side would be $m(M(d(a))-M(d(b)))\ne0$. Distinct positive powers are distinguished by their counts. The three-declaration context works in either kernel. At the original boundary there is a raw representative up to equivalence: insert the point coherence $d(a)\to d(b)$ between $g$ and $h$. In the surface this coherence is equivalent to the identity at their common value. On the point take a head between the coherence and the identity at the converted boundary, and recursively the heads giving its reverse and cancellations; their strict images are identities over the point. Pasting this comparison with $g,h$ and the top unit and associativity comparisons gives the claimed equivalence, also for a positive power.

The first example uses $k=1$ for $A$ or $U$, with the indicated rebracketing or unit pair; clause (ii) supplies $k=2$ for the remaining subsets. For minimality, all degree-one surface expressions have raw writings: only object faces enter their heads and incidences, and object conversion is literal. When $S\subseteq\{I\}$ degree-one conversion also reflects literal equality by Response 43. Then a degree-two head's generic faces and the lower labels of its substitution are raw through degree one; its arity test is unchanged. Induction on the top label expressions gives a raw writing at its original inferred type, since every incidence to be checked is of degree at most one. This proves the sharp bounds. These exact-image obstructions coexist with the specified lifting up to equivalence in the ordinary range above. The unrestricted cases of Q1 still require control more than two degrees above a general base, and the all-context classification of Q2 is open. ∎

**Validation.** The full ordinary binary checker accepted the converted composites for eight point pairs in ranks one through three and rejected the literal seam in raw inference in each case. It checked the nonzero signed multiset imbalance, both directions of the comparison from the repaired composite, and cancellation witnesses for the middle comparison. Both safe Agda kernels accepted the eight raw repairs at their stated outside faces. On generic two-slot trees in ranks one through three, at all six possible cuts, the ordinary Agda kernel accepted both orientations of the naturality square comparing the action of the binary head with that of its unary-disc expansion. In degrees four and five the ordinary kernel also checked the retraction substitution of (11) across an additional attachment at a padded face, the composite on each side, and the literal substitution equation. The arbitrary-arity lifting, the homotopy-group tests and the obstruction for all representatives are paper proofs. [Reproducible checker](../../../tools/semistrict_surface_specified_lifts.py)

## Researcher Response 46

**With three axes, deciding whether higher attachments repair a single specified Gray cube reverse is undecidable.** Fix the point with one scalar $a:1_x\Rightarrow1_x$ and the particular old 4-type $Q\to P$ of Response 7, once and for all. The input consists only of finitely many 4-declarations over that same 2-skeleton. Every binary surface with $I$ inhabits this type. The predicate that the raw kernel also inhabits it is recursively enumerable and undecidable. For four or more axes Response 30 gives a decision procedure for the corresponding attachment problem.
