### Making the globes fibrant over the forgetful maps

Use the spaces $W_S(U)=|K(S;U)|$, their subcomplex inclusions and intersections, permutation actions and maps forgetting names from Response 47. Write $R$ for the following functor on such inverse diagrams of spaces. At $(S,U)$ put $N_A=\lim_{T\subsetneq S}(RA)_T(U|_T)$ and define
$$
 (RA)_S(U)= A_S(U)\times_{N_A,\mathrm{ev}_0}N_A^{[0,1]} .
$$
Here the map into $N_A$ uses the constant-path inclusions on all smaller sets. The projection to the matching object is evaluation at one. Use compactly generated spaces and then singular simplicial sets. This functor commutes with finite limits and with taking paths, by induction in cardinality. For example the inverse limit and the path-space functor in its definition both preserve pullbacks. It preserves inclusions and their intersections at each $S$, compatibly with restriction of constraints. Each constant-path inclusion into $RA$ is an objectwise equivalence, by contracting a path to its first endpoint.

We use the following relative form. If $A\to B$ is objectwise a fibration on singular complexes, including on fixed points for every stabilizer subgroup, then
$$
 (RA)_S\longrightarrow (RB)_S\times_{N_B}N_A                 \tag{15}
$$
is a fibration on singular complexes with the same fixed-point property. In a horn lift first lift the $A_S$ coordinate over the given $B_S$ simplex. The path in $N_A$ now has both endpoints prescribed and its image path in $N_B$ specified; its horn is already given. Fill by the path boundary fibration associated to $N_A\to N_B$. That map is a fibration by the smaller instances of (15): adjoin the proper subsets in increasing size, using pullbacks of the relative matching fibrations. On fixed points adjoin one orbit of subsets at a time and use the fibration for the stabilizer of a representative. This proves the induction. If the objectwise maps are also weak equivalences on those fixed points, the same argument with simplex boundaries proves that (15) is a trivial fibration. Equivalently use the constant-path equivalences and induction on the matching maps. Products with the simplicial interval and the topological path spaces agree here under singular adjunction on simplices by triangulation of prisms.

At each rank take the **globes first**: $G_j=R(M_j(W))$, with the Moore globes and normalized-path topology of Response 57. All strict standard operations, identities, the stage sweeps at a specified *fraction* of total time, and top concatenation act pointwise on this construction. Indeed on Moore globes these particular operations are continuous and commute with postcomposition by continuous maps, retaining the input durations. Their definitions therefore act also on the external paths in $R$, satisfying its endpoint conditions. A cell has a duration for each of its Moore coordinates, including each value along the external paths. The projection to a smaller set retains that matching coordinate. The standard insertion and whole-tuple identities (14) hold in every coordinate.

For $j\ge1$ there are two fibrations with different uses. First $G_j\to B_jG$ is relatively fibrant in the sense of (15), by the ordinary Moore boundary fibration and preservation of the boundary limits. Secondly put $D_jG$ equal to the space of **normalized, fixed-interval paths** in $G_{j-1}$ at fixed lower globular boundary (all paths for $j=1$). Forgetting just the *outermost* Moore length gives a map
$$ d_j:G_j\longrightarrow D_jG. $$
This map is relatively **trivially fibrant** in (15). Before $R$, a boundary lift over a simplex of normalized paths only needs an outer length: extend the given nonnegative length and make it positive off the simplex boundary, with length one possible if there is no boundary. At its zeroes the prescribed normalized path is constant. The same proof applies to fixed points since the action does not change length. Now $R$ commutes with the fixed-boundary path construction, which is a finite limit using powers by the interval. Apply (15) to this objectwise trivial fibration. These arguments use Kan lifting on singular complexes; the ordinary Moore boundary fibration itself uses only the square lift and the positive extension of length in Response 57.

### Insertion operations compatible with all projections

Hereafter take singular complexes. A normalized track over a simplex means a map out of its prism, so the singular complex of $D_jG$ is also the indicated vertical path object with simplicial interval. Choose the following two functions of their input data once at each rank. Both choices respect inclusions of constraints, forgetting and permutations.

* Transport a starting $j$-cell along an external interval of its globular boundary. Prescribe identity transport whenever the start is an identity and the boundary stays diagonal. For the lifting proof let $K$ be the simplicial set of compatible starting cells and boundary paths, and $K_0$ the subcomplex of the prescribed identity case. At each $S$, induct from smaller constraint subcomplexes to larger ones, with proper subsets of $S$ already treated. On $K\times\{0\}$ the lift is given; on $(K_0\cup K')\times\Delta^1$ it is prescribed, where $K'$ is the union from the smaller constraint subcomplexes. The prescriptions agree on intersections by induction. This domain includes into $K\times\Delta^1$ by a trivial cofibration, the pushout product of a monomorphism with the endpoint inclusion. Use (15) for the globe boundary fibration with the matching transport already specified.
* Given a reference $j$-cell $a$ and a normalized track $c\in D_jG$, select $q(a,c)\in G_j$ over $c$. If $c=d_ja$, prescribe $a$. If $a$ is an identity and $c$ is constant at $v$, prescribe $1_v$. The two prescriptions agree on their intersection, even when $v$ is unrelated to the face of $a$ in the second prescription alone. These conditions define subcomplexes of the input product. Adjoin the smaller constraint subcomplexes as above and lift over the selected track and its previously specified matching lifts. This time the domain inclusion need only be a monomorphism, since (15) for $d_j$ is a trivial fibration.

For both choices the input limits and imposed loci restrict correctly on constraint intersections. Work on one permutation orbit of indices at a time. The inclusions in the second lift are monomorphisms also on fixed simplices, and in the first lift the endpoint-cylinder inclusion is a trivial cofibration on fixed simplices. The relative fibrations on all fixed points give the equivariant lifts (use equivariant simplicial cells $H/L\times\Delta^m$). The prescriptions are required on the actual union of the translates of all previously treated subcomplexes, so the choices extend those already made.

A rank-$k$ head now has an action on $G$. For the first $k-1$ stages of its tuple sweep, transport the initial identity along the two lower evaluated face tracks by the first choice. During the final stage the source is stationary and the target gives a normalized vertical track $c$. This is true on the entire diagram: the negative $(k-1)$-section is fixed by this stage as a tuple, and the shared lower boundary is fixed. Take $a$ to be the **standard** rank-$k$ evaluation on the full tuple, including identity padding. Append $q(a,c)$ to the transported cell by strict top concatenation. Its endpoints are the two required faces. This construction works simplexwise: operations on a swept simplex are evaluated on the prism, and for a constant stage the prism factors through projection. There is no need to choose a single common duration for its different projections.

The proof of the equations is direct. At a standard head all early transports are identities and $c=d_ka$ by the serial formula, so the output is exactly $a$. At a generic endo all early transports are identities, $a$ is an identity by the height bound, and $c$ is constant. The second prescription for $q$ gives the desired identity. The standard disc selects its cell. In an insertion the whole earlier sweep and both face evaluations agree by (14) and lower-rank substitution. The final reference $a$ also agrees by standard insertion, and the track $c$ agrees by the target calculation on the same sweep. Thus $q$ and concatenation give the same answer at the two heads. Equal generic types give the same evaluated faces. This verifies the published formation and equality rules including conversion. It simultaneously gives strict algebra homomorphisms on all the indicated diagram arrows.

For the raw comparison use $F_j$, the fixed-interval globes. The map normalizing all parameter levels in $M_j(W)\to F_j(W)$ is objectwise a trivial fibration **relative to globular boundary** by the prism and length extension of Response 57, also on fixed points. Applying (15) to that relative map gives a trivial fibration with both the globular and the proper-subset matching data prescribed. The target $R(F_j(W))=F_j(RW)$ has the usual fundamental raw operations, natural in continuous maps. Lift this action to a raw action on $G$ over it, fixing constant tuples. Induct in rank, subset size and constraint inclusion as above, prescribing the matching result and the action on smaller subcomplexes. The joint relative trivial fibration extends over the entire input along the resulting monomorphism. Interpolate the lifted action to the reduct of the published action, keeping the common constant values. On each unconstrained tuple space and on each subcomplex and fixed-point intersection the pointwise stages give a deformation to its constant-tuples section. For the interpolation relative to subcomplexes, include the endpoints, the constants through the interval and the full prisms on the smaller subcomplexes. This inclusion is a trivial cofibration: adding the smaller subcomplexes to the constant-tuples locus preserves its equivalence by finite gluing, since each has its own constant-tuples equivalence and so do their intersections. On fixed points a fixed simplex in a translated subcomplex belongs also to the intersection of its translates. Take the pushout product with the interval boundary and lift using (15) for the globe boundary. Evaluation at the ends of the resulting raw path action lifts globes at their exact boundaries as in Response 57. This represents, in the homotopy theory of diagrams, the original diagram of spaces $W$, including its permutations and restrictions.

### Recovering the integral obstruction

At every split symbol with its pair constraints, $G_S(U)\to\lim_{T\subsetneq S}G_T(U|_T)$ is a globular trivial fibration on vertices. To verify this, in each degree $j$ the constant inclusion $W\to M_j(W)$ is a natural equivalence: normalized time gives the comparison with fixed-interval globes, and those are maps from a contractible disc. The $R$-replacement at that degree computes the homotopy matching limit, by its matching fibrations. The block section of Response 47 proves the equivalence from $W_S(U)$ to this homotopy limit at a split. Thus $G_{S,j}(U)\to N_{G_j}$ is an objectwise weak equivalence there in every degree. The relative matching map at the globular boundary is a fibration by (15). It is a weak equivalence by induction on globe rank, since the map on its boundary limits is a trivial fibration by the lower ranks. Surjectivity on vertices gives the claimed exact lift, including for a symbol of local degree zero.

Use the published-insertion free presentation of the fixed hom $B(S)$ in Response 47. At zero or one name send the terminal component to the constant identity tuple on the unique configuration-poset vertex; the action fixes these constants even though the target allows other durations. At a split symbol place its already interpreted faces in the indicated constraint, with its proper restrictions giving the matching cell. The preceding trivial fibration lifts it there. Grouped operations use the local published action; consequently the induction on generators gives strict algebra maps on fixed homs compatible with actual deletion of names. The pair-bound and permutation checks are those of the free presentation and do not depend on truncation of the target. Composing with the raw map now gives the maps $F_S:\operatorname{Conf}_S(\mathbb R^n)\to\operatorname{Conf}_S(\mathbb R^n)$ of Response 47 with **untruncated** target, for insertion as well. The common degree on the pair sphere is $\pm1$: for any prime dividing it all positive fibre cohomology maps of the $p$-particle cyclic Borel construction vanish mod $p$, whereas the base map is identity. This contradicts the first nonzero differential to the base row, which exists by freeness and finite-dimensionality of the configuration quotient. Pair classes generate integral cohomology, so every $F_S$ is an integral homology equivalence, and a homotopy equivalence for $n\ge3$ by simple connectivity. Distinguish occurrences in a proposed filler and apply this equivalence to its difference sphere. Its vanishing supplies a raw filler at the given faces, which folds to the original names. Ranks one and two and the lower poles were settled in the graph and planar calculations. This proves the claim for insertion and its binary subsurface. [Split presentation, matching equivalence, integral detection and generator transport](#researcher-response-47)

It follows also that the criterion on $X_n(D)$ in Response 37 reduces to conservativity on $D$ alone for published insertion and separately for binary $\{A,U\}$. Both are conservative in all ranks and degrees on the single-input and single-output scalar rule systems of Response 38. The proof uses the configuration decomposition of the last fixed hom; arbitrary directed attachments in Q1 and the general context classification in Q2 still require further arguments. ∎

**Validation.** At the product matching map the clock test evaluated both projected pieces of concatenated paths over the external interval, with independent zero or positive lengths. Direct concatenation agreed with the separately elapsed times in all 4,050 evaluations, including 72 input patterns with unequal projected clocks. A normalized track varying inside a parameter interval with both prescribed lengths zero received a continuous Moore lift at those endpoints. The existing finite checks also verified 2,799 standard insertions, 34,191 carrier restrictions, and the three-particle syllepsis restriction in Rice's insertion checker. The relative trivial fibration and its all-dimensional application are mathematical arguments. [Clock and insertion checker](../../../tools/semistrict_surface_moore_insertion.py) · [Carrier and three-particle checker](../../../tools/semistrict_surface_configuration_carriers.py)

## Researcher Response 60

**At one Gray scalar, the fixed old 4-type from the first crossing to the second crossing on three occurrences has undecidable surface inhabitation.** The input varies only finitely many symmetric, equal-length 4-declarations at width three. The reverse of the native cube is supplied throughout. The raw answer at the specified type is always negative, and raw inhabitation in the whole presentation is decidable. This holds for each binary surface containing $I$. It simultaneously attains the minimum possible number of scalar names, temporal width and maximum face length for this undecidability in the scalar class: width two is decidable by Response 53, and at length zero both faces have identity strict value and already admit a raw comparison. The scalar and the fixed crossing terms are those of Response 46. [Literal three-scalar crossing loops](#researcher-response-46) · [One-scalar signed order](#researcher-response-53)

**The group detected by an uncoloured boundary.** Write $x=\sigma_1$, $y=\sigma_2$, $P=xyx$, $Q=xy$, $Z=P^2=Q^3$. Then $B_3/\langle Z\rangle=M=C_2*C_3$, generated by $p,q$, the images of $P,Q$. Take any finite quotient *presentation* $H=\langle p,q\mid p^2,q^3,R\rangle$; the group $H$ may be infinite. Map $B_3$ to $H\times\mathbb Z$ by modular projection to $H$ and ordinary crossing exponent $e$ to $\mathbb Z$. As in Response 58, its kernel admits a finite effective list of weight-zero normal generators. Explicitly, lift $R$ to words in $P,Q$, set $g=\gcd(6,e(r):r\in R)$, and take a Bézout product $D$ of these lifts and $Z$ with $e(D)=g$. Impose $[D,P]=[D,Q]=1$, $r=D^{e(r)/g}$ for each lifted relator, and $Z=D^{6/g}$. These equations vanish under the map. Conversely the kernel of the first projection is normally generated by the lifted relators and $Z$, because quotienting $B_3$ by $Z$ gives $M$. Each such normal generator has become a power of the central $D$, so exponent zero forces the remaining power to vanish. Thus these equations realize precisely the claimed kernel.

Clear each signed equation with positive full twists and prepend another full twist to both sides as in Response 58. Declare both directions at the three-scalar input, and add the bare cube return. The signed comparison at width three is exactly equality in the image of $B_3\to H\times\mathbb Z$ by Response 53: all signed contexts are now allowed, so conjugation is by $B_3$ itself. All extra declarations have equal face lengths at least six. In particular two positive braids of the same crossing length are surface-comparable exactly when their modular images agree in $H$. The two single crossings have the same exponent, so the fixed comparison is equivalent to equality of their images in $H$. Since $x,y$ generate $B_3$ and are conjugate, their images agree precisely when $H$ is cyclic. Neither positive braid equality nor a declared step applies between the length-one words $x,y$ in the raw kernel.

**Undecidability at literal face length one.** Triviality of finite presentations of quotients of $M$ is undecidable (Schupp; also stated explicitly by Kapovich–Schupp). The abelianization of the given $H$ is cyclic of computable order $g$: abelianization of $M$ sends $p,q$ to $3,2$ modulo six. If $g>1$, $H$ is nontrivial. If $g=1$, it is trivial exactly when it is cyclic. A surface decision at the displayed fixed boundary would therefore decide triviality. This reduction applies even with the promise $g=1$. In the affirmative case *every* equal-length pair at width three compares on the surface; in the negative case the two single crossings already separate it from that condition. Thus recognizing whether the signed width-three test reduces to crossing length alone is also undecidable. [Schupp, *Embeddings into Simple Groups*](https://doi.org/10.1112/jlms/s2-13.1.90) · [Kapovich–Schupp, *Random quotients of the modular group*, p. 7](https://arxiv.org/pdf/math/0604343)

There is also one fixed finite homogeneous presentation on the single scalar with undecidable old-boundary surface inhabitation at width three. Miller–Schupp's embedding of any finitely presented group into a finitely presented quotient of $M$ supplies such an $H$ with unsolvable word problem. For a word $w(P,Q)$, failure of $g\mid e(w)$ already implies a negative answer in $H$. Otherwise clear the weight-zero word $wD^{-e(w)/g}$ and test its positive pair. This decides whether $w=1$ in $H$ if a surface solver exists. Raw reachability remains finite search at the given length and width. The nonexistence of a recursive denominator bound from Response 58 consequently holds with a single scalar name. [Miller–Schupp embedding as stated by Kapovich–Schupp, p. 7](https://arxiv.org/pdf/math/0604343)

**Validation.** Run `python3 tools/semistrict_surface_modular_encoding.py --agda /tmp/SingleCrossingGroupTest.agda` and `agda --safe -i src -i /tmp /tmp/SingleCrossingGroupTest.agda`. The compiler checked 2,046 word queries in six finite permutation representations, including the $(2,3,5)$ generators on five letters, both answers to the fixed test, and all four divisors $g$ of six. Agda 2.8.0 checked the two positional crossing loops and their literal old arrow type. To generate the homogeneous declarations and query for the perfect triangle-group example, run `python3 tools/semistrict_surface_modular_encoding.py --relators pqpqpqpqpq --word pq`. The shared kernel encoder also passed the 1,023 named-pure queries of Response 58. [Modular quotient compiler and old-boundary check](../../../tools/semistrict_surface_modular_encoding.py)

## Researcher Response 61

**Both raw and surface inhabitation at any given three-strand old 4-type are decidable if the nontrivial placed declarations strictly increase a common additive grading.** The test for such a grading is itself effective and finds one whenever one exists. This applies to all scalar presentations of Response 56, with arbitrary names and any of the four binary surfaces containing $I$. On three occurrences of one scalar the hypothesis says precisely that all declarations changing the braid strictly increase crossing length, or all strictly decrease it. Braid-preserving declarations of any kind may coexist with them, and the native cube need not have a raw return. On named strands the theorem also covers mixed signs and certain nontrivial equal-length rules. It decides whether each queried type is a failure of reflection. Response 60 shows why allowing arbitrary equal-length changes would preclude such a decision. [Signed order on named braids](#researcher-response-56) · [Undecidability without the grading restriction](#researcher-response-60)

**Computing the grading at the specified input.** Fix its orbit of name orders $O$. Let $J$ be the group of braids returning to the starting order, and let $G=J/\langle Z\rangle$, with $Z$ the pure full twist. Work in the modular groupoid generated by $p,q$ at the orders of $O$, with $p^2=q^3=1$. Here $p$ reverses the list, $q$ rotates it, and $\sigma_1=q^{-1}p$, $\sigma_2=pq^{-1}$ modulo $Z$. For each $p$- or $q$-orbit introduce a vertex joined to its orders. A free cyclic orbit gives just this star: between any two of its orders there is a unique arrow using only that cyclic generator. A singleton retains its cyclic group at the star vertex. Contract a spanning tree in the resulting graph. Its other edges give free generators, and the only remaining relations are the orders of the retained cyclic generators. This computes the label of each typed path, with the tree paths supplying its two connectors to the base order. For input multiplicities $3$, $2+1$ and $1+1+1$, respectively, the resulting $G$ is $C_2*C_3$, $C_2*\mathbb Z$ and $\mathbb Z*\mathbb Z$.

Place each applicable declaration $l\to r$ at each allowed position and input order. Record its defect $r l^{-1}$ with the indicated tree connector. Its integer vector consists of the ordinary crossing-number difference and the exponent sums of its free generators in $G$. This list detects every rational additive grading on $J$: from any homomorphism $J\to\mathbb Q$ deduct a multiple of the crossing exponent to kill $Z$, whose exponent is six. The remainder descends to $G$, where the finite factors contribute zero. Discard exactly the braid identities, recognized by trivial projection and zero crossing exponent. Solve $h\cdot v_i\ge1$ over the rationals for the other defect vectors. Exact elimination and clearing denominators produce an integral grading when feasible. With no nonidentity defects only braid equality can hold on the surface.

**From a length bound to a decision.** For old words $b,c$ the $h$-difference $B$ of $cb^{-1}$ bounds the sum of the positive costs of the used defects. Thus enumerate finitely many multisets of defects at that cost and with total crossing exponent $|c|-|b|$. Their order can be varied freely in a product of conjugacy classes. In $G$ test whether the image of $cb^{-1}$ is a product of conjugates of the selected defects. Every choice of conjugators in $G$ lifts to signed typed contexts, using the tree connectors. The discrepancy of two lifted loops with the same modular image is a power of $Z$; the crossing-exponent check forces that power to be zero. Response 56 gives exactly the required surface judgment. For the raw test the same cost bounds the number of nonneutral steps; each can increase word length by at most the greatest positive declared increase. All neutral steps preserve length. Search this finite bounded graph using the native forward cube and the actual positive rules at their named inputs. This decides raw inhabitation even if the grading uses a negative crossing weight. [Independent general decision theorem for equations in virtually free groups](https://doi.org/10.1112/jtopol/jtq010)

**A finite surface test for the conjugate products.** Use the cyclic word of the inverse desired product on one boundary circle and those of the selected factors on the others. Such words are realized by a map of an oriented sphere with holes to a classifying space for $G$ precisely when the required conjugate-product equation holds. In the free product of cyclic groups above this has an elementary decision. Arrange the letters on their respective circles. Partition them by generator into pairs of opposite signs at an infinite cyclic factor, arbitrary pairs at $C_2$, and opposite pairs or triples of equal sign at $C_3$. Make a vertex for each packet, with an edge to each of its letters. Some partition and cyclic orders at the packet vertices must give a planar graph with the prescribed cyclic orders at the boundary circles.

For necessity, realize the free product by factor spaces joined along a tree to a common point. Cut a surface map over interior points of the joining edges, in general position. Each factor region has boundary letters of total exponent zero in that cyclic factor, since the surface is orientable and the factor group abelian. Connect its letters by an embedded star in the region (use a regular neighbourhood of a tree joining the designated boundary intervals). In the resulting cyclic order peel off adjacent opposite signs, then pairs at $C_2$ or triples at $C_3$ from any remaining uniform finite-factor sequence. The smaller stars can be drawn disjointly in the same neighbourhood. Conversely thicken a planar drawing of the packet stars, leaving the letter intervals on the boundary circles. Each packet extends over its sector since its boundary product is trivial in its cyclic group; map the rest to the common point, using the joining paths at the sectors. Disconnected graphs embed in disjoint discs on the same sphere. The solver checks planarity for each finite choice by the Euler characteristic of its oriented ribbon graph, using the cycles of the product of its two vertex rotations as faces. Empty boundary words put no restriction on the test.

**Validation.** Run `python3 tools/semistrict_surface_monotone_braids.py`. The solver compared 1,922 surface judgments for insertion and its reverse with the independent three-strand subword theorem of Response 54; another 961 raw judgments after cube repair agreed with the same decision. It checked 310 named path and braid comparisons and tested all three forms of the stabilizer. For a mixed-sign decision, run `python3 tools/semistrict_surface_monotone_braids.py --at ABC --faces 22 11 --rule AB::11 --rule BC:11:`. The indicated comparison exists and its reverse is absent. For a failure of reflection use `--at ABC --faces '' 11 --rule BA::11` and repeat with `--raw`: the surface transports the square insertion to the opposite input order, whereas the raw empty word has no applicable declaration at that order. [Grading, planar equation solver and raw search](../../../tools/semistrict_surface_monotone_braids.py)

## Researcher Response 62

**A single signed insertion of a crossing power always lifts to a single positive insertion at every width.** Take any finite set of scalar names and supply raw returns for all bare cubes. Suppose a width-two insertion of the empty word to $\sigma^d$, $d>0$, is available raw at **both orders** of every unordered name pair on which it is used. For two distinct names $d$ must be even for these faces to be parallel. If old positive face braids $b,c$ are related in the surface by one such insertion in arbitrary signed contexts and braid equalities, there is a raw comparison at those faces, using that insertion between positive words. The dual assertion holds for deletion. This supplies a computable certificate even when the displayed words have no literal insertion taking one to the other. The condition on both orders is sharp: the opposite-order example in Response 56 already fails with one signed square insertion.

In particular, suppose the only braid-changing declarations are such one-sided power insertions and let $d_0$ be the least exponent among them. **All widths reflect at crossing-length gaps below $2d_0$**, regardless of the face lengths themselves. More generally this holds at any gap admitting no sum of two or more allowed exponents. For insertion of one crossing, Response 54's four-strand witness has gap two. It now attains both the minimum failing width and the minimum number of insertions in a surface comparison, across all widths. [Named signed order](#researcher-response-56) · [Two-insertion failure](#researcher-response-54)

Let the defect be $cb^{-1}=L\sigma_i^d L^{-1}$. Remove the greatest common positive right divisor $R$ of $b,c$, writing $b=B R$ and $c=C R$. This is the right orthogonal splitting $C B^{-1}$ in the positive braid monoid. Godelle, Kaplan and Teicher's Theorem 1.1 says that the splitting of a conjugate of a positive standard-generator power has precisely the form $(B\sigma_j^d) B^{-1}$. By uniqueness $C=B\sigma_j^d$. Thus an actual positive spelling of $b$ admits the indicated insertion between $B$ and $R$. Conversely that positive certificate visibly gives a signed one. This argument uses the spherical Artin theorem; cancellativity alone would not force the insertion to be available after cancelling a suffix. [Godelle–Kaplan–Teicher, *Conjugacy in Artin groups*, Theorem 1.1 and Proposition 2.2](https://arxiv.org/pdf/math/0312188)

The unordered pair of strand occurrences used in this positive spelling agrees with the pair in the signed insertion. For odd $d$ the permutation of the defect is exactly the transposition of those original strands. For even $d$ the defect is pure: its pairwise linking numbers vanish off that pair, with $d/2$ on the pair. Indeed the two parts of the conjugating path traverse opposite crossings of the same original strands, since the central power in the conjugation has trivial permutation. This argument tracks *occurrences* even when several have the same name. Evaluating the prescribed input names therefore makes the positive insertion available at the actual ordered pair after $B$. Positive equalities fitting the two resulting words lift raw by the cube returns and old-face fitting. Finally, the crossing exponent counts the positive cost of every signed insertion, so a gap below $2d_0$ permits at most one; gap zero uses only positive braid equality.

**Validation.** Run `python3 tools/semistrict_surface_single_power.py`. The solver strips common terminal atoms until the positive fraction is right-coprime, then searches for the indicated power directly. It compared 8,910 splitting tests with exhaustive insertion over all positive spellings at widths three and four. Another 270 parallel boundaries agreed with the independent signed equation solver of Response 61. For example, `--at AAA --faces 1,2 2,1,2,1 --pair AA` produces an insertion of $22$ before $12$, followed by the positive equality $2212=2121$. The solver uses finite spelling enumeration to make its certificate inspectable; the cited theorem also gives a double-reversing recognition algorithm. [Orthogonal-splitting solver](../../../tools/semistrict_surface_single_power.py)

## Researcher Response 63

**Arbitrary attachments over a scalar skeleton.** Let $B$ consist of vertices and finitely many ordinary $n$-scalars, $n\ge3$, at any raw writings of the iterated point boundaries. There is an ordinary binary $\{A,U\}$ model $Z$ and a raw weak equivalence $H_B\to UZ$. Consequently, if the first declaration of $X$ above $B$ has degree $h>n$, binary $\{A,U\}$ is conservative through degree $h+1$ in $X$. This allows consecutive declarations in degrees $n+1,n+2$, arbitrary attaching terms involving several different scalar occurrences, and further declarations above them. If only the surface equations on data generated through degree $h-1$ are imposed as in Response 32, conservativity holds in all degrees of $X$. [Relative attachment theorem](#researcher-response-32) · [Fixed-hom probes](#researcher-response-59)

### Composing configurations on disjoint sets of occurrences

Fix one vertex. Use the complete graph spaces $W(S;U)$ of Response 47 on finite sets $S$ with pair constraints $U$, including the unconstrained $W(S)$. If $S$ is partitioned into the sets $S_a$ at the depth-$n$ positions of a tree, graphs on the $S_a$ have a block composite. Keep their internal edges, concatenate their orders in planar order, and set an exterior edge to the signed level of the first divergence of its two positions, numbered from zero. This is monotone in the input graphs and acts on realizations by the product isomorphism for nerves. Empty blocks contribute no edges. Forgetting names commutes strictly with the operation. For a binary split at cut $i<n$ the exterior level is $i$; both bracketings at the same cut give the very same graph on three blocks. In degree $n$ the empty configuration is its two-sided unit. [Berger, Example 1.15(b)](https://math.univ-cotedazur.fr/~cberger/config.pdf#page=6) · [Balteanu–Fiedorowicz–Schwänzl–Vogt, complete graph comparison](https://doi.org/10.1016/S0001-8708(03)00065-3)

We need a particular version of the replacement in Response 59 which admits *external products on different name sets*. For an inverse diagram $D$ on subsets, take $(RD)(S)$ to consist of a map $\Delta^p_{\rm top}\to D(T_p)$ for every chain $S\supseteq T_0\supseteq\cdots\supseteq T_p$. Omitting a vertex gives the corresponding face; for the last vertex compose the shorter-chain map with restriction to $T_p$. Repetitions use the ordinary simplex degeneracy. Restriction retains the chains in the smaller subset. This construction preserves limits and commutes with taking paths in compactly generated spaces. The constant cone from $D(S)$ is an equivalence with inverse evaluation on the vertex $S$. Explicitly, prefix $S$ to each chain and give it weight $u$, multiplying the old barycentric weights by $1-u$. This contracts the cone to the restrictions of its value at $S$.

Here too the relative matching map (15) is a fibration on singular complexes if $D\to D'$ is an objectwise fibration there, and is trivial if those fibrations are trivial. To lift a horn with the proper-subset data given, fill the nondegenerate chains starting at $S$ by increasing length. On a chain simplex its boundary is already prescribed using the faces just described. The parameter horn times that simplex together with the full parameter simplex times its boundary includes by a trivial cofibration (triangulate the product). Lift into $D(T_p)$ over the prescribed map into $D'(T_p)$. For trivial fibrations do the same with a parameter boundary. Equivariantly, choose an orbit of chains and fill with its stabilizer, using the fixed-point hypothesis of (15). This also proves absolute matching fibrancy for singular spaces. Inclusions of constraints induce subcomplexes and preserve intersections.

Take $G_{l,S}(U)=\operatorname{Sing}(R M_l(W(-;U|-)))(S)$, using the Moore globes of Response 57. In particular clocks are taken *before* replacement. These complexes have the relative globe-boundary fibrations, normalized-clock comparisons and split matching trivial fibrations proved in Response 59: its proofs use (15), preservation of the path limits, and the constant-cone equivalence, all established for this $R$. At a split the last assertion applies when there are at least two nonempty $S_a$ and the only bounds on exterior edges are the principal bounds of their split. Internals may have any constraints. The graph section above gives the required equivalence with the proper-subset homotopy limit, including on restrictions where only one block survives. [Moore globes](#researcher-response-57) · [Split matching calculation](#researcher-response-47) · [Relative globular fibrations](#researcher-response-59)

To combine labelled configurations on a chain in $S$, evaluate each operand on the chain intersected throughout with its block $S_a$, at the same barycentric coordinates. Repeated vertices act by degeneracy. Combine the resulting entries pointwise. This respects every face of the chain, including the last face by naturality under erasure. In rank $n$ use the block composite. At a binary cut $i<n$, lift that binary operation to Moore paths by running the left path with the right input at its source, then the right path with the left input at its target, using the previously defined operation one level lower. Iterate for higher Moore globes. At a cut $i\ge n$, the operands are in the same labelled component after matching their common face. Use ordinary Moore concatenation in dimension $i+1$, and the same serial lift above it. Pad a lower operand with zero-duration identities to the required degree. The faces of these operations are the specified binary boundaries: at the concatenation cut they are the two outside endpoints, and above a cut they are obtained by applying the lower operation to the respective endpoints. At a lower-dimensional identity cut its supporting cell supplies the two matching endpoints.

These formulas give strict associativity at a common cut, both units, and distribution of identities. For associativity above a cut, both sides have the sum of the three durations, and at each time the same operand runs with the same other two held fixed. Induct one level down at those values. For units an iterated identity has zero outer duration and acts by the unit one level down. Two identity inputs have zero total duration and output the identity of the lower composite. These verifications include the whiskerings. A projection may send a moving operand to a positive-duration constant path on the empty configuration. It still occupies time in the serial composite. Only the actual identity has to contribute zero time.

### Extending the other heads with their prescribed boundaries

Use a single cell in each degree below $n$. In degree $n+l$ use the vertices of $G_{l,S}$ for all finite occurrence sets coloured by the given scalar names, modulo simultaneous relabelling of the occurrences. A bijection fixing a point of $W(S)$ fixes each vertex in its supporting simplex and hence the linear order of a graph; it is the identity. Evaluation on the vertex $S$ therefore gives free relabelling on $G_{0,S}$. Each higher cell lifts uniquely to named occurrences once a lift of its iterated $n$-source is specified. For a tuple on a globular tree, choose disjoint names in each depth-$n$ position and propagate along its local tree. Its $n$-variables form the successive objects of that local tree. The possible paths of transports of names agree: parallel $(n+1)$-labels joined by an $(n+2)$-label have the same name transport by the globular faces of a named lift. After these identifications the generating transports form a string. Thus the named tuple exists, uniquely up to the independent initial bijections. This is the transport-class calculation of Response 33. We construct operations on such named tuples, in fact on their simplices, respecting restriction and bijection. They then give operations on the stated quotient. [Transport along an arity](#researcher-response-33)

Use the block operation for every rank-$n$ head, using no blocks when its height is smaller than $n$. Above $n$ prescribe the binary operations just constructed and identities. The remaining choices can be indexed at each tree by its generic strict tag and the already interpreted boundary functions on all named tuples. In particular any head with identity tag and globally equal face functions can be prescribed as the identity on their common value. This criterion is applied to the functions before specializing to any particular tuple or erasure. The identity prescription and binary prescriptions are compatible: a genuine two-slot binary head has a nonidentity total on its generic tree, and its tree specifies its two dimensions and cut. On a one-slot disc with the variable tag and its actual boundary functions prescribe the variable. This is consistent with the rank-$n$ block formula and disjoint from the two-slot case. A change of bound type preserving the index leaves each prescription unchanged. This extra choice gives the unary disc law of Response 15 as well.

We give the induction for every remaining head of rank $k=n+l$, $l>0$. Require that if the labels within each position satisfy specified internal pair constraints, the output satisfies those constraints and all the exterior split bounds. The corresponding statement for subexpressions uses only the positions visited by their strict $n$-sources. Here are details of the positional check used in the induction. The strict $n$-source uses one occurrence from each visited transport class, at most once. When tracing two names through a substituted boundary, names from one used position travel together until evaluation inside its labels. Names from different positions pass to different operands at a split of depth at least their original divergence and in the same order if the depths agree. This is the signed pair calculation of Responses 35 and 47, applied to the intervals visited by the strict $n$-source on the tree of the substitution. A full head visits all positions, by collapsing each transport class to one variable. Thus lower boundary evaluations land in the required carrier: internal bounds pass through grouped operands and subsequent splits, and an exterior bound persists by transitivity of signed-level comparison. This verification is pointwise on each subset chain. It uses no normal-form assertion about the joint binary laws. For a prescribed binary operation the same bound follows directly by block composition or by serial motion within that carrier. An identity inherits its constraints from its face. [Positional separation](#researcher-response-35) · [Bounds under substitution](#researcher-response-47)

First treat the *static* tuples: all higher labels in each depth-$n$ position are iterated identities on a single configuration for that position. With at most one nonempty block prescribe the iterated identity on its configuration, using the empty configuration if necessary. Both lower faces are exactly the required faces. More explicitly, a rank-$n$ subexpression on this tuple selects the one configuration if it visits that position, and the empty one otherwise. Higher subexpressions give its iterated identities: every nested tuple is again static with at most one occupied position by the at-most-once assertion, the previously assigned general heads have this property, and the binary operations on identities preserve it. For the two full faces both visit the same positions. This establishes the single-block condition inductively, including for static simplices.

With at least two nonempty blocks, fix the internal constraints on the static inputs and use precisely the principal exterior bounds in the output $U$. The lower faces in $G_{l-1,S}(U)$ are known. The static outputs on all proper subsets are known by induction on $|S|$ at this same rank. Their constraints are the restrictions of $U$ and their globular faces are the restrictions of the given faces. They determine a map from the static input complex to the target of the joint globular-boundary and proper-subset matching map. This map lifts by the split trivial fibration above. To make these choices simultaneously, extend from smaller input constraint subcomplexes first, prescribing their outputs in the larger carrier. They meet in their constraint intersections, of which there are finitely many at fixed $S$. The remaining domain inclusion is monic. A stabilizer preserving the tuple's positions acts freely on its static input simplices, since each nonempty block contains a freely named configuration. Fill one orbit of nondegenerate simplices at a time. This proves existence of compatible simplicial operations on static tuples. Forced binary and identity prescriptions already give such operations on their entire domains and require no new lift.

For a general tuple, sweep each local tree *within its own depth-$n$ position* by the stages of Response 57, using a common fraction of each position's stage duration. At each chain coordinate use the local Moore lengths there, so the whole deformation is compatible with erasure, degeneracies and each internal constraint. It starts at the static tuple of the initial configurations and ends at the given tuple. Along these stages the two interpreted lower faces are paths of a common globular boundary in $G_S(U)$. Transport the chosen static filler along that boundary. Use the simultaneous transport of Response 59, now requiring also constant transport on every constant boundary path. This extra prescription is on a simplicial subcomplex and agrees with diagonal identity transport on their intersection. The same relative cylinder lift against (15) extends their union and the choices for smaller constraints. The resulting operation respects erasures, permutations and all the carrier inclusions. On a static tuple the sweep is constant and gives exactly the chosen static filler. This closes the rank induction; use increasing cardinality within a rank for the static lifts.

The simplicial operations have the evaluated lower faces by construction. Substitution is evaluation on an evaluated tuple. The word laws (i) hold by the strict binary calculations; for (ii) the equal functions on the generic word faces and the identity strict tag give the identity prescription. The tag is an identity since a globular tree has no nonidentity strict endomorphism. Congruence on generic types is respected by the indexing of the choices. These observations verify the typing and equality rules together, including converted generic faces. Taking vertices and the colour quotient gives the asserted binary model. Choices on the named inputs were independent of their colours, so recolouring and forgetting all occurrences of specified colours are homomorphisms.

### Comparing the whole scalar skeleton and changing base

Assign each generator its singleton configuration. The lower faces are unique in the model. In its named $n$-hom component on $S$, the raw operations on higher globes are the operations on the local tree in one depth-$n$ position (suspend the local head $n$ times). On constant tuples they give the iterated identities on the configuration, by the single-block static prescription. The raw comparison of Response 59 now applies to these operations. In detail, normalize the Moore clocks to map to the fixed-interval globes on $RW(S)$; lift the fundamental raw action across this relative trivial fibration preserving the constant tuples. Interpolate this action with the present one on the same $G$ using the relative globe-boundary fibrations. The relative input domain is the two ends of the interval and the constant tuples throughout. Its inclusion is a trivial cofibration because the local tuple sweep retracts the whole tuple to that section, including on constraint subcomplexes and fixed points. As in Response 59 this gives a span of raw comparisons lifting literal globes and representing the diagram of spaces $W(S)$. In particular all cells above $n$ in the component are weakly invertible.

On once-occurring distinct colours, the map from the raw scalar skeleton consequently induces a homotopy endomorphism of the configuration diagram, with its permutations and forgetful maps. In the source this is the free little-$n$-cubes calculation of Response 33. In the target the comparison just constructed uses the same maps, and the image of a forgotten singleton is precisely the empty $n$-cell. The two-name degree must be $\pm1$ by the prime argument of Response 47: if $p$ divides it the map kills all positive-degree mod-$p$ fibre cohomology on $p$ names (generated by pairs). In the cyclic homotopy-orbit spectral sequence it must preserve the first nonzero differential into the bottom row, where it acts as identity. Such a differential exists by finite dimensionality of the free quotient. This contradiction rules out every prime. Pair generation now gives an integral cohomology isomorphism on each component; simple connectivity for $n\ge3$ and finite CW type give an equivalence.

For a fixed multiset of colours take the quotient by the product of the symmetric groups on its equal-colour occurrences. On the source the free little-cubes formula is the homotopy-orbit formula. On the target each named globe, once its source naming is fixed, has a unique lift from the quotient. The raw comparison span above descends: a globe lifting problem for either projection from the interpolating raw algebra can be lifted to the named problem by naming its starting configuration, matching the names at the other faces by its given target globe and boundary. For a 1-globe the named terminal point can equivalently be chosen by the target lift; if the specified upstairs endpoint projects to it in the quotient, its unique representative over the named endpoint is used. For higher globes the prescribed parallelism already propagates the same naming along the incident edges. The projections act over equivariant base spaces with free action on objects, so these choices commute at shared faces. The fixed-interval globe algebra on $RW(S)$ modulo this finite group is the fundamental globe algebra of the quotient: the free finite action on this Hausdorff space is a covering action, and each disk lifts once a basepoint lift is given. Equivalently the local raw comparison computes the homotopy orbits. The map for repeated colours is induced from the once-occurring diagram by recolouring, which on the source also takes the indicated homotopy orbits. Hence it is an equivalence for every multiset, including the empty one.

Finally both $n$-fold endomorphism objects have precisely these components, with the empty one the only invertible multiset. Below $n$ the source has the single point pole up to equivalence in each degree (the delooping calculation); the target has one literal cell at each of these degrees. Inducting back through these homs gives full faithfulness and essential surjectivity of the raw assignment. Noncanonical attaching terms on the point induce the same derived scalar assignment by transport along the contractible point data as in Response 35, and distinct vertices give a disjoint union. This proves $H_B\to UZ$ a raw weak equivalence. Apply Response 32 with $r=h-1$, first to its unrestricted two-degree theorem and then to its all-degree theorem on equations on the lower generators. This yields both attachment conclusions.

The same bound applies to mixed attachments above **iterated labelled scalar constructions** $X_n(D)$ of arbitrary ranks $n\ge2$, starting with any finite ordinary 2-computad. More generally the base hypothesis on $D$ is a raw weak equivalence into a binary $\{A,U\}$ model with unary disc removal. For $n=2$ this is Response 37. For $n\ge3$ decorate each named occurrence of the present configuration model with an object of the label model, and each higher globe with a label globe on each occurrence. Read a head on each name by the weight-one $n$-fold cut of Response 37. The unique named lift above specifies the occurrence at both ends; parallel higher faces have the same transport. The cut-substitution calculation applies since the strict $n$-source uses each class once. Thus raw and surface evaluation commute with taking each label coordinate, including the disc law. On the hom with all occurrences distinguished the raw comparison is the product of the configuration comparison just proved and the label comparisons: suspending a label operation $n$ times cuts back to that operation, while forgetting labels gives the original configuration operation. This is precisely the product in the raw realization of $X_n(D)$ from Response 36. At repeated occurrences choose orders at the two ends; the hom decomposes over the permutation carrying one order to the other, with its corresponding labelled comparison on each summand by unique occurrence lifting. This proves descent as in Response 37 and gives the raw weak equivalence into the decorated model. The initial 2-computads admit the disc-trivial binary model of Response 26, and the construction preserves the hypothesis for the next iteration. Response 32 therefore applies over any such base with first additional generator of degree $h$ strictly above its generating dimension. [Raw labelled realization](#researcher-response-36) · [Weight-one cuts and descent](#researcher-response-37)

The extension of heads here prescribes binary words and their identity coherences; the unrestricted published insertion question above these attachments remains open. ∎

**Validation.** The block checker verified 3,456 erasures of configurations at mixed cuts and 6,144 associativity comparisons on barycentrically weighted subset chains, including chains with repeated intersections. It also checked the 6,144 last-face equations, 12,288 unit equations, and the positive empty clock distinguished from the zero-duration unit. In a context with one 3-scalar and four 4-generators on its composite with itself, Rice's raw checker accepted a fitted 5-generator at a reassociated, unpadded source. The direct use of the generator requires conversion; published SUA accepted that use and rejected the reversed type. The fit itself is a raw coherence on the three-slot arity and the conversion uses only binary associativity and units. These are finite checks; the compatible extensions and weak equivalence are proved above. [Configuration block and attachment checker](../../../tools/semistrict_surface_configuration_blocks.py)
